I came in because things at work have been getting… I don’t know, coordinated, I guess. I work in transportation, and lately dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system, not just in the normal way they say they do. It’s like every time I adjust something or take a slightly different route, there’s some little comment over the radio, like they’re hinting that they saw it or that they’re keeping notes.

And people keep telling me it’s just oversight, it’s just policy, but it doesn’t feel like that. Certain drivers seem to be reporting on me too, like they’ll say something casual, but it lines up too closely with what I did earlier or where I was. I don’t think so… not like a voice talking to me when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like the work stuff started feeling connected over time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know if I’d call it one big upsetting event, like a death in the family or something sudden like that. It wasn’t like one day something horrible happened and then the next day everything changed.

The main thing was around eight months ago, when they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. That’s when it started building, I think. At first I was just trying to handle the extra responsibility and not mess it up, because even though they called it “temporary,” people still treat you like you’re supposed to know everything and catch every little problem. And I was more visible, you know? More people were coming to me, watching what I did, asking why I made certain calls.

So if you’re asking what happened shortly before it started, I’d say that role change was the big thing. Not exactly an “event” like a crisis, but it put me in the middle of everything, and since then it’s just sort of built up over the months.

Yeah… I have. I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do it, so part of me wants to say I’m managing, but honestly it’s not like before.

I’ve been falling behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because those are the kinds of things I used to be able to just knock out. Now I’ll start one, then I’m double-checking things, or rereading what I wrote, or trying to figure out if I worded something wrong, and then suddenly I’m behind. And when I give instructions to drivers, I guess I’m not being clear enough, because some of them have complained that what I’m saying is confusing or that I changed something halfway through. I don’t mean to do that, but I can hear myself over-explaining sometimes, and then I lose the main point.

And it’s not just work, really. At home my husband has had to take over more than he should. Like meals, phone calls, basic planning stuff — things I used to keep track of. I’ll think I’m going to handle it, and then I either forget or I get stuck trying to decide what to do first. So yes, it’s affecting my job, and I guess it’s affecting more than my job too.

I don’t mean to do that, but I can hear myself over-explaining sometimes, and then I lose the main point.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, really. At home my husband has had to take over more than he should. Like meals, phone calls, basic planning stuff — things I used to keep track of. So yes, it’s affecting my job, and I guess it’s affecting more than my job too.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you had a period where you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. But I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “much less sleep.” Like, are you asking if I stayed up because I couldn’t sleep, or if I didn’t need sleep and felt great? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… people have said that. Mostly at work, honestly. The drivers have complained a few times that when I’m giving instructions, I’m not making sense or I’m saying it in a confusing way. And I hate hearing that, because in my head I feel like I’m trying to be careful and explain it right, but then I guess I over-explain or change how I’m saying it halfway through, and they’re left not knowing what I actually meant.

It’s also showing up in the route reports. I’ve fallen behind on them because I keep rereading and trying to make the wording clear, and then it takes forever. Those reports used to be pretty routine for me, so it’s embarrassing.

At home, my husband notices too, not always about “speech” exactly, but about me not keeping up with planning things. He’s had to take over meals, phone calls, basic organizing stuff because I’ll start trying to explain or decide something and then I don’t really get it done. So… yes, I think other people have noticed, even if I didn’t want to admit it at first.

I know how the system works, I’ve been around it, and this feels different. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone, because I’m tired of feeling like I’m being watched and talked about while I’m just trying to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it’s possible you might be mistaken about your coworkers and dispatch tracking or hinting about you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess anything is possible in the abstract, right? Like, I know people say that to me — that I might be reading too much into normal dispatch stuff, or that compliance is just doing their job. I don’t know. People keep telling me that. But honestly, I can tell something is going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices or sounds that other people couldn’t hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a voice talking to me when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean. Like I might think I heard my name or a comment, and then I’ll listen harder, but it’s hard to tell with all the background noise.\n\nBut sitting at home or somewhere quiet, I’m not hearing voices or sounds that nobody else hears, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that I’m trying to make sense of what people are saying around me, especially over the radio or at work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling that your coworkers and dispatch are watching you or hinting about you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about two months where it’s felt really clear to me. I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do it, so part of me wants to say I’m managing, but honestly it’s not like before.\n\nI’ve been falling behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because those are the kinds of things I used to be able to just knock out. Now I’ll start one, then I’m double-checking things, or rereading what I wrote, or trying to figure out if I worded something wrong, and then suddenly I’m behind. Not exactly like… thoughts being put in my head or taken out, I don’t think. I don’t want to say yes to that if that’s not what I mean.

But I do feel like people know more than they should, if that makes sense. Like at work, with dispatch and compliance, I’m here because I really think they’ve been tracking my route changes through the computer system. I know how the system works, I’ve been around it, and this feels different. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone, because I’m tired of feeling like I’m being watched and talked about while I’m just trying to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it’s possible you might be mistaken about your coworkers and dispatch tracking or hinting about you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess anything is possible in the abstract, right? Like, I know people say that to me — that I might be reading too much into normal dispatch stuff, or that compliance is just doing their job. But when I’m actually there, when I’m on the route and I make a change and then there’s a little comment over the radio that lines up with it, it doesn’t feel like a coincidence.\n\nIt’s not just one thing. If it were one time, I’d probably shrug it off. But it’s the pattern. The computer system logs route changes, I know that, but this feels more directed than that. Like they’re watching what I do and then certain drivers say these casual things that are too specific, like they’re reporting back or letting me know they know. So… could I be mistaken? I don’t know. People keep telling me that. But honestly, I can tell something is going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices or sounds that other people couldn’t hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a voice talking to me when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean. I mean, at work there are a lot of sounds all the time — radios, engine noise, people talking over each other, dispatch cutting in and out — so sometimes I’ll hear something and I’m not sure if I caught it right. Like I might think I heard my name or a comment, and then I’ll listen harder, but it’s hard to tell with all the background noise.\n\nBut sitting at home or somewhere quiet, I’m not hearing voices or sounds that nobody else hears, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that I’m trying to make sense of what people are saying around me, especially over the radio or at work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling that your coworkers and dispatch are watching you or hinting about you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about two months where it’s felt really clear to me. Like before that, maybe there were little things I brushed off, or I thought, “Okay, maybe dispatch is just being annoying,” but for the past couple months it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nThat’s when the computer tracking and the radio comments started feeling connected, like not just normal work monitoring. I’d make a route change or do something a little different, and then there’d be some comment that seemed too close to what I had just done. So, yeah… about two months that I’ve really felt like they’re watching me and hinting about it.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you used any drugs or stimulants like methamphetamine, cocaine, or extra Adderall?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back… I don’t really have a clear thing I can point to, like, “Oh, right before this started I took something and then everything changed.” When you say “extra Adderall,” do you mean like prescribed medication taken differently, or taking something not prescribed? I just don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nBut as far as what I can remember, there wasn’t some obvious drug or stimulant thing right before this started that explains it to me. It’s more like the work stuff started feeling connected over time.\n\nDoctor: Did a major stress or upsetting life event happen shortly before this started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it one big upsetting event, like a death in the family or something sudden like that. It wasn’t like one day something horrible happened and then the next day everything changed.\n\nThe main thing was around eight months ago, when they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. That’s when it started building, I think. At first I was just trying to handle the extra responsibility and not mess it up, because even though they called it “temporary,” people still treat you like you’re supposed to know everything and catch every little problem. And I was more visible, you know? More people were coming to me, watching what I did, asking why I made certain calls.\n\nSo if you’re asking what happened shortly before it started, I’d say that role change was the big thing. Not exactly an “event” like a crisis, but it put me in the middle of everything, and since then it’s just sort of built up over the months.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been having trouble doing your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do it, so part of me wants to say I’m managing, but honestly it’s not like before.\n\nI’ve been falling behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because those are the kinds of things I used to be able to just knock out. Now I’ll start one, then I’m double-checking things, or rereading what I wrote, or trying to figure out if I worded something wrong, and then suddenly I’m behind. And when I give instructions to drivers, I guess I’m not being clear enough, because some of them have complained that what I’m saying is confusing or that I changed something halfway through. I don’t mean to do that, but I can hear myself over-explaining sometimes, and then I lose the main point.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, really. At home my husband has had to take over more than he should. Like meals, phone calls, basic planning stuff — things I used to keep track of. I’ll think I’m going to handle it, and then I either forget or I get stuck trying to decide what to do first. So yes, it’s affecting my job, and I guess it’s affecting more than my job too.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you had a period where you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I’ve had times where I was wired from stress or couldn’t settle down, but “unusually energetic” and needing way less sleep, like I was fine on just a couple hours and full of energy the next day — I don’t really recognize that.\n\nIf anything, when I don’t sleep well, I feel worn out and kind of scattered, not like I have extra energy. But I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “much less sleep.” Like, are you asking if I stayed up because I couldn’t sleep, or if I didn’t need sleep and felt great? Because the second one, no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Before these concerns started, did you have any new medical problem, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can think of. I didn’t have, like, an accident or hit my head or anything that stands out before all this started.\n\nAnd no seizures that I know of. I’m trying to think if there was anything medical around then, because sometimes you don’t connect things until someone asks, but nothing really comes to mind. I wasn’t suddenly diagnosed with something new or in the hospital or anything like that. It was more just… life and work getting heavier, at least from how I remember it.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech or the way you explain things has become hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people have said that. Mostly at work, honestly. The drivers have complained a few times that when I’m giving instructions, I’m not making sense or I’m saying it in a confusing way. And I hate hearing that, because in my head I feel like I’m trying to be careful and explain it right, but then I guess I over-explain or change how I’m saying it halfway through, and they’re left not knowing what I actually meant.\n\nIt’s also showing up in the route reports. I’ve fallen behind on them because I keep rereading and trying to make the wording clear, and then it takes forever. Those reports used to be pretty routine for me, so it’s embarrassing.\n\nAt home, my husband notices too, not always about “speech” exactly, but about me not keeping up with planning things. He’s had to take over meals, phone calls, basic organizing stuff because I’ll start trying to explain or decide something and then I don’t really get it done. So… yes, I think other people have noticed, even if I didn’t want to admit it at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt as if your thoughts were being put into your mind, taken out of your mind, or broadcast so other people could know them?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like… thoughts being put in my head or taken out, I don’t think. I don’t want to say yes to that if that’s not what I mean.\n\nBut I do feel like people know more than they should, if that makes sense. Like at work, with dispatch and compliance, I’m here because I really think they’ve been tracking my route changes through the computer system. And then there are these little comments over the radio — not direct, always, but enough that I can tell they’re about me or about what I did.\n\nPeople keep telling me it’s just normal oversight, like it’s routine monitoring, but it doesn’t feel routine. It feels more targeted. Like certain drivers are reporting back on me, and then dispatch already knows what I’m thinking or planning to do before I even explain it. Not “broadcast” like everyone can hear my thoughts out loud, but… it does feel like they’re watching and piecing together what’s in my head from the routes and the radio chatter. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure. I mean, I know sometimes when I’m talking I’ll start with one thing and then I’ll add too much around it, like background or an example, and then I realize I’m not answering in a straight line anymore. But I don’t know if it feels like my thoughts are “jumping quickly,” exactly.

Other people might experience that as me jumping around, I guess. From my side it feels like I’m trying to be thorough, not like ideas are racing so fast I can’t hold onto one.

So… I don’t know. Maybe sometimes I have trouble staying on one point, but I haven’t really thought of it as my thoughts jumping quickly.

I’ll think I’m going to handle it, and then I either forget or I get stuck trying to decide what to do first. I’ve had times where I was wired from stress or couldn’t settle down, but “unusually energetic” and needing way less sleep, like I was fine on just a couple hours and full of energy the next day — I don’t really recognize that.\n\nIf anything, when I don’t sleep well, I feel worn out and kind of scattered, not like I have extra energy. But I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “much less sleep.” Like, are you asking if I stayed up because I couldn’t sleep, or if I didn’t need sleep and felt great? Because the second one, no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Before these concerns started, did you have any new medical problem, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can think of. I didn’t have, like, an accident or hit my head or anything that stands out before all this started.\n\nAnd no seizures that I know of. I’m trying to think if there was anything medical around then, because sometimes you don’t connect things until someone asks, but nothing really comes to mind. I wasn’t suddenly diagnosed with something new or in the hospital or anything like that. It was more just… life and work getting heavier, at least from how I remember it.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech or the way you explain things has become hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people have said that. Mostly at work, honestly. The drivers have complained a few times that when I’m giving instructions, I’m not making sense or I’m saying it in a confusing way. And I hate hearing that, because in my head I feel like I’m trying to be careful and explain it right, but then I guess I over-explain or change how I’m saying it halfway through, and they’re left not knowing what I actually meant.\n\nIt’s also showing up in the route reports. I’ve fallen behind on them because I keep rereading and trying to make the wording clear, and then it takes forever. Those reports used to be pretty routine for me, so it’s embarrassing.\n\nAt home, my husband notices too, not always about “speech” exactly, but about me not keeping up with planning things. He’s had to take over meals, phone calls, basic organizing stuff because I’ll start trying to explain or decide something and then I don’t really get it done. So… yes, I think other people have noticed, even if I didn’t want to admit it at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt as if your thoughts were being put into your mind, taken out of your mind, or broadcast so other people could know them?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like… thoughts being put in my head or taken out, I don’t think. I don’t want to say yes to that if that’s not what I mean.\n\nBut I do feel like people know more than they should, if that makes sense. Like at work, with dispatch and compliance, I’m here because I really think they’ve been tracking my route changes through the computer system. And then there are these little comments over the radio — not direct, always, but enough that I can tell they’re about me or about what I did.\n\nPeople keep telling me it’s just normal oversight, like it’s routine monitoring, but it doesn’t feel routine. It feels more targeted. Like certain drivers are reporting back on me, and then dispatch already knows what I’m thinking or planning to do before I even explain it. Not “broadcast” like everyone can hear my thoughts out loud, but… it does feel like they’re watching and piecing together what’s in my head from the routes and the radio chatter. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do your thoughts ever jump from one idea to another so quickly that you have trouble staying on one point?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know sometimes when I’m talking I’ll start with one thing and then I’ll add too much around it, like background or an example, and then I realize I’m not answering in a straight line anymore. But I don’t know if it feels like my thoughts are “jumping quickly,” exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying to make sure I explain it the right way, so I’ll go back and clarify something, and then I remember another detail that seems important, and then I can kind of lose the main point. Other people might experience that as me jumping around, I guess. From my side it feels like I’m trying to be thorough, not like ideas are racing so fast I can’t hold onto one.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe sometimes I have trouble staying on one point, but I haven’t really thought of it as my thoughts jumping quickly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying it, because it makes me sound like I’m just being lazy or like I don’t care about people, and I do care. But I’ve definitely pulled back.

I used to sing with the choir, and that was something I actually looked forward to. It wasn’t just singing, it was seeing the same people every week and having a reason to get out and be part of something. And now when it’s time to go, I just… don’t feel it. I’ll think about getting ready, and then it feels like too much, or like I can’t make myself care enough to move. Same with the softball team. We used to have people over sometimes, and I’d help plan food or where everyone would sit, and now the idea of having them in the house feels exhausting before it even starts.

Most days after my shift I come home and I’ll sit at the kitchen table with the planner open. Like I’m trying to be normal and organized — dinner, phone calls, whatever needs doing — but I just stare at it. I can see the things that need to happen, but I can’t seem to start them. Then time goes by and nothing gets done, and my husband ends up picking up the slack.

So yes, I guess I have lost interest, or maybe lost the ability to get myself to do the things I used to do. It’s not exactly that I hate those things now. It’s more like the feeling that used to push me toward them is just not there most of the time.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F20.9, F22, F43.2, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because things at work have been getting… I don’t know, coordinated, I guess. I work in transportation, and lately dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system, not just in the normal way they say they do. It’s like every time I adjust something or take a slightly different route, there’s some little comment over the radio, like they’re hinting that they saw it or that they’re keeping notes.\n\nAnd people keep telling me it’s just oversight, it’s just policy, but it doesn’t feel like that. Certain drivers seem to be reporting on me too, like they’ll say something casual, but it lines up too closely with what I did earlier or where I was. I know how the system works, I’ve been around it, and this feels different. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone, because I’m tired of feeling like I’m being watched and talked about while I’m just trying to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it’s possible you might be mistaken about your coworkers and dispatch tracking or hinting about you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess anything is possible in the abstract, right? Like, I know people say that to me — that I might be reading too much into normal dispatch stuff, or that compliance is just doing their job. But when I’m actually there, when I’m on the route and I make a change and then there’s a little comment over the radio that lines up with it, it doesn’t feel like a coincidence.\n\nIt’s not just one thing. If it were one time, I’d probably shrug it off. But it’s the pattern. The computer system logs route changes, I know that, but this feels more directed than that. Like they’re watching what I do and then certain drivers say these casual things that are too specific, like they’re reporting back or letting me know they know. So… could I be mistaken? I don’t know. People keep telling me that. But honestly, I can tell something is going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices or sounds that other people couldn’t hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a voice talking to me when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean. I mean, at work there are a lot of sounds all the time — radios, engine noise, people talking over each other, dispatch cutting in and out — so sometimes I’ll hear something and I’m not sure if I caught it right. Like I might think I heard my name or a comment, and then I’ll listen harder, but it’s hard to tell with all the background noise.\n\nBut sitting at home or somewhere quiet, I’m not hearing voices or sounds that nobody else hears, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that I’m trying to make sense of what people are saying around me, especially over the radio or at work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling that your coworkers and dispatch are watching you or hinting about you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about two months where it’s felt really clear to me. Like before that, maybe there were little things I brushed off, or I thought, “Okay, maybe dispatch is just being annoying,” but for the past couple months it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nThat’s when the computer tracking and the radio comments started feeling connected, like not just normal work monitoring. I’d make a route change or do something a little different, and then there’d be some comment that seemed too close to what I had just done. So, yeah… about two months that I’ve really felt like they’re watching me and hinting about it.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you used any drugs or stimulants like methamphetamine, cocaine, or extra Adderall?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back… I don’t really have a clear thing I can point to, like, “Oh, right before this started I took something and then everything changed.” When you say “extra Adderall,” do you mean like prescribed medication taken differently, or taking something not prescribed? I just don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nBut as far as what I can remember, there wasn’t some obvious drug or stimulant thing right before this started that explains it to me. It’s more like the work stuff started feeling connected over time.\n\nDoctor: Did a major stress or upsetting life event happen shortly before this started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it one big upsetting event, like a death in the family or something sudden like that. It wasn’t like one day something horrible happened and then the next day everything changed.\n\nThe main thing was around eight months ago, when they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. That’s when it started building, I think. At first I was just trying to handle the extra responsibility and not mess it up, because even though they called it “temporary,” people still treat you like you’re supposed to know everything and catch every little problem. And I was more visible, you know? More people were coming to me, watching what I did, asking why I made certain calls.\n\nSo if you’re asking what happened shortly before it started, I’d say that role change was the big thing. Not exactly an “event” like a crisis, but it put me in the middle of everything, and since then it’s just sort of built up over the months.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been having trouble doing your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do it, so part of me wants to say I’m managing, but honestly it’s not like before.\n\nI’ve been falling behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because those are the kinds of things I used to be able to just knock out. Now I’ll start one, then I’m double-checking things, or rereading what I wrote, or trying to figure out if I worded something wrong, and then suddenly I’m behind. And when I give instructions to drivers, I guess I’m not being clear enough, because some of them have complained that what I’m saying is confusing or that I changed something halfway through. I don’t mean to do that, but I can hear myself over-explaining sometimes, and then I lose the main point.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, really. At home my husband has had to take over more than he should. Like meals, phone calls, basic planning stuff — things I used to keep track of. I’ll think I’m going to handle it, and then I either forget or I get stuck trying to decide what to do first. So yes, it’s affecting my job, and I guess it’s affecting more than my job too.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you had a period where you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I’ve had times where I was wired from stress or couldn’t settle down, but “unusually energetic” and needing way less sleep, like I was fine on just a couple hours and full of energy the next day — I don’t really recognize that.\n\nIf anything, when I don’t sleep well, I feel worn out and kind of scattered, not like I have extra energy. But I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “much less sleep.” Like, are you asking if I stayed up because I couldn’t sleep, or if I didn’t need sleep and felt great? Because the second one, no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Before these concerns started, did you have any new medical problem, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can think of. I didn’t have, like, an accident or hit my head or anything that stands out before all this started.\n\nAnd no seizures that I know of. I’m trying to think if there was anything medical around then, because sometimes you don’t connect things until someone asks, but nothing really comes to mind. I wasn’t suddenly diagnosed with something new or in the hospital or anything like that. It was more just… life and work getting heavier, at least from how I remember it.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech or the way you explain things has become hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people have said that. Mostly at work, honestly. The drivers have complained a few times that when I’m giving instructions, I’m not making sense or I’m saying it in a confusing way. And I hate hearing that, because in my head I feel like I’m trying to be careful and explain it right, but then I guess I over-explain or change how I’m saying it halfway through, and they’re left not knowing what I actually meant.\n\nIt’s also showing up in the route reports. I’ve fallen behind on them because I keep rereading and trying to make the wording clear, and then it takes forever. Those reports used to be pretty routine for me, so it’s embarrassing.\n\nAt home, my husband notices too, not always about “speech” exactly, but about me not keeping up with planning things. He’s had to take over meals, phone calls, basic organizing stuff because I’ll start trying to explain or decide something and then I don’t really get it done. So… yes, I think other people have noticed, even if I didn’t want to admit it at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt as if your thoughts were being put into your mind, taken out of your mind, or broadcast so other people could know them?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like… thoughts being put in my head or taken out, I don’t think. I don’t want to say yes to that if that’s not what I mean.\n\nBut I do feel like people know more than they should, if that makes sense. Like at work, with dispatch and compliance, I’m here because I really think they’ve been tracking my route changes through the computer system. And then there are these little comments over the radio — not direct, always, but enough that I can tell they’re about me or about what I did.\n\nPeople keep telling me it’s just normal oversight, like it’s routine monitoring, but it doesn’t feel routine. It feels more targeted. Like certain drivers are reporting back on me, and then dispatch already knows what I’m thinking or planning to do before I even explain it. Not “broadcast” like everyone can hear my thoughts out loud, but… it does feel like they’re watching and piecing together what’s in my head from the routes and the radio chatter. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do your thoughts ever jump from one idea to another so quickly that you have trouble staying on one point?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know sometimes when I’m talking I’ll start with one thing and then I’ll add too much around it, like background or an example, and then I realize I’m not answering in a straight line anymore. But I don’t know if it feels like my thoughts are “jumping quickly,” exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying to make sure I explain it the right way, so I’ll go back and clarify something, and then I remember another detail that seems important, and then I can kind of lose the main point. Other people might experience that as me jumping around, I guess. From my side it feels like I’m trying to be thorough, not like ideas are racing so fast I can’t hold onto one.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe sometimes I have trouble staying on one point, but I haven’t really thought of it as my thoughts jumping quickly.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you lost interest in being around other people or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying it, because it makes me sound like I’m just being lazy or like I don’t care about people, and I do care. But I’ve definitely pulled back.\n\nI used to sing with the choir, and that was something I actually looked forward to. It wasn’t just singing, it was seeing the same people every week and having a reason to get out and be part of something. And now when it’s time to go, I just… don’t feel it. I’ll think about getting ready, and then it feels like too much, or like I can’t make myself care enough to move. Same with the softball team. We used to have people over sometimes, and I’d help plan food or where everyone would sit, and now the idea of having them in the house feels exhausting before it even starts.\n\nMost days after my shift I come home and I’ll sit at the kitchen table with the planner open. Like I’m trying to be normal and organized — dinner, phone calls, whatever needs doing — but I just stare at it. I can see the things that need to happen, but I can’t seem to start them. Then time goes by and nothing gets done, and my husband ends up picking up the slack.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest, or maybe lost the ability to get myself to do the things I used to do. It’s not exactly that I hate those things now. It’s more like the feeling that used to push me toward them is just not there most of the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know if anyone has said it exactly that way, like “your voice is flat” or “your face is flat.” My husband maybe says I seem distant, or like I’m not really there in the same way, but I don’t always know what my face is doing.

What I do notice is more that I don’t have the same… lift, I guess. Like with choir, I used to enjoy singing, and now I don’t even feel like going. Or having the softball team over — that used to be something I could get into, planning it and being around everybody, and now it just feels like too much.

Most days after my shift, I’ll sit at the kitchen table with the planner open in front of me, and I’ll know I’m supposed to start dinner or make calls or take care of whatever’s written there, but I just sit there. I’m not trying to look blank or sound uninterested. It’s more like I can’t get the feeling to start things, and maybe that comes across on my face or in my voice.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.9, F23, F22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because things at work have been getting… I don’t know, coordinated, I guess. I work in transportation, and lately dispatch and compliance have been tracking my route changes through the computer system, not just in the normal way they say they do. It’s like every time I adjust something or take a slightly different route, there’s some little comment over the radio, like they’re hinting that they saw it or that they’re keeping notes.\n\nAnd people keep telling me it’s just oversight, it’s just policy, but it doesn’t feel like that. Certain drivers seem to be reporting on me too, like they’ll say something casual, but it lines up too closely with what I did earlier or where I was. I know how the system works, I’ve been around it, and this feels different. It’s been bothering me enough that I figured I should talk to someone, because I’m tired of feeling like I’m being watched and talked about while I’m just trying to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it’s possible you might be mistaken about your coworkers and dispatch tracking or hinting about you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess anything is possible in the abstract, right? Like, I know people say that to me — that I might be reading too much into normal dispatch stuff, or that compliance is just doing their job. But when I’m actually there, when I’m on the route and I make a change and then there’s a little comment over the radio that lines up with it, it doesn’t feel like a coincidence.\n\nIt’s not just one thing. If it were one time, I’d probably shrug it off. But it’s the pattern. The computer system logs route changes, I know that, but this feels more directed than that. Like they’re watching what I do and then certain drivers say these casual things that are too specific, like they’re reporting back or letting me know they know. So… could I be mistaken? I don’t know. People keep telling me that. But honestly, I can tell something is going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you heard voices or sounds that other people couldn’t hear?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a voice talking to me when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean. I mean, at work there are a lot of sounds all the time — radios, engine noise, people talking over each other, dispatch cutting in and out — so sometimes I’ll hear something and I’m not sure if I caught it right. Like I might think I heard my name or a comment, and then I’ll listen harder, but it’s hard to tell with all the background noise.\n\nBut sitting at home or somewhere quiet, I’m not hearing voices or sounds that nobody else hears, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that I’m trying to make sense of what people are saying around me, especially over the radio or at work.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling that your coworkers and dispatch are watching you or hinting about you?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say about two months where it’s felt really clear to me. Like before that, maybe there were little things I brushed off, or I thought, “Okay, maybe dispatch is just being annoying,” but for the past couple months it’s been harder to ignore.\n\nThat’s when the computer tracking and the radio comments started feeling connected, like not just normal work monitoring. I’d make a route change or do something a little different, and then there’d be some comment that seemed too close to what I had just done. So, yeah… about two months that I’ve really felt like they’re watching me and hinting about it.\n\nDoctor: Before this started, had you used any drugs or stimulants like methamphetamine, cocaine, or extra Adderall?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think back… I don’t really have a clear thing I can point to, like, “Oh, right before this started I took something and then everything changed.” When you say “extra Adderall,” do you mean like prescribed medication taken differently, or taking something not prescribed? I just don’t want to answer it wrong.\n\nBut as far as what I can remember, there wasn’t some obvious drug or stimulant thing right before this started that explains it to me. It’s more like the work stuff started feeling connected over time.\n\nDoctor: Did a major stress or upsetting life event happen shortly before this started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it one big upsetting event, like a death in the family or something sudden like that. It wasn’t like one day something horrible happened and then the next day everything changed.\n\nThe main thing was around eight months ago, when they put me in that temporary dispatch lead role. That’s when it started building, I think. At first I was just trying to handle the extra responsibility and not mess it up, because even though they called it “temporary,” people still treat you like you’re supposed to know everything and catch every little problem. And I was more visible, you know? More people were coming to me, watching what I did, asking why I made certain calls.\n\nSo if you’re asking what happened shortly before it started, I’d say that role change was the big thing. Not exactly an “event” like a crisis, but it put me in the middle of everything, and since then it’s just sort of built up over the months.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been having trouble doing your job?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I’m still showing up and trying to do it, so part of me wants to say I’m managing, but honestly it’s not like before.\n\nI’ve been falling behind on the route reports, which is embarrassing because those are the kinds of things I used to be able to just knock out. Now I’ll start one, then I’m double-checking things, or rereading what I wrote, or trying to figure out if I worded something wrong, and then suddenly I’m behind. And when I give instructions to drivers, I guess I’m not being clear enough, because some of them have complained that what I’m saying is confusing or that I changed something halfway through. I don’t mean to do that, but I can hear myself over-explaining sometimes, and then I lose the main point.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, really. At home my husband has had to take over more than he should. Like meals, phone calls, basic planning stuff — things I used to keep track of. I’ll think I’m going to handle it, and then I either forget or I get stuck trying to decide what to do first. So yes, it’s affecting my job, and I guess it’s affecting more than my job too.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you had a period where you felt unusually energetic and needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. I’ve had times where I was wired from stress or couldn’t settle down, but “unusually energetic” and needing way less sleep, like I was fine on just a couple hours and full of energy the next day — I don’t really recognize that.\n\nIf anything, when I don’t sleep well, I feel worn out and kind of scattered, not like I have extra energy. But I guess I’m not totally sure what counts as “much less sleep.” Like, are you asking if I stayed up because I couldn’t sleep, or if I didn’t need sleep and felt great? Because the second one, no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Before these concerns started, did you have any new medical problem, head injury, or seizure?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, not that I can think of. I didn’t have, like, an accident or hit my head or anything that stands out before all this started.\n\nAnd no seizures that I know of. I’m trying to think if there was anything medical around then, because sometimes you don’t connect things until someone asks, but nothing really comes to mind. I wasn’t suddenly diagnosed with something new or in the hospital or anything like that. It was more just… life and work getting heavier, at least from how I remember it.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you that your speech or the way you explain things has become hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Yeah… people have said that. Mostly at work, honestly. The drivers have complained a few times that when I’m giving instructions, I’m not making sense or I’m saying it in a confusing way. And I hate hearing that, because in my head I feel like I’m trying to be careful and explain it right, but then I guess I over-explain or change how I’m saying it halfway through, and they’re left not knowing what I actually meant.\n\nIt’s also showing up in the route reports. I’ve fallen behind on them because I keep rereading and trying to make the wording clear, and then it takes forever. Those reports used to be pretty routine for me, so it’s embarrassing.\n\nAt home, my husband notices too, not always about “speech” exactly, but about me not keeping up with planning things. He’s had to take over meals, phone calls, basic organizing stuff because I’ll start trying to explain or decide something and then I don’t really get it done. So… yes, I think other people have noticed, even if I didn’t want to admit it at first.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt as if your thoughts were being put into your mind, taken out of your mind, or broadcast so other people could know them?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like… thoughts being put in my head or taken out, I don’t think. I don’t want to say yes to that if that’s not what I mean.\n\nBut I do feel like people know more than they should, if that makes sense. Like at work, with dispatch and compliance, I’m here because I really think they’ve been tracking my route changes through the computer system. And then there are these little comments over the radio — not direct, always, but enough that I can tell they’re about me or about what I did.\n\nPeople keep telling me it’s just normal oversight, like it’s routine monitoring, but it doesn’t feel routine. It feels more targeted. Like certain drivers are reporting back on me, and then dispatch already knows what I’m thinking or planning to do before I even explain it. Not “broadcast” like everyone can hear my thoughts out loud, but… it does feel like they’re watching and piecing together what’s in my head from the routes and the radio chatter. That’s the part that bothers me.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do your thoughts ever jump from one idea to another so quickly that you have trouble staying on one point?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know sometimes when I’m talking I’ll start with one thing and then I’ll add too much around it, like background or an example, and then I realize I’m not answering in a straight line anymore. But I don’t know if it feels like my thoughts are “jumping quickly,” exactly.\n\nIt’s more like I’m trying to make sure I explain it the right way, so I’ll go back and clarify something, and then I remember another detail that seems important, and then I can kind of lose the main point. Other people might experience that as me jumping around, I guess. From my side it feels like I’m trying to be thorough, not like ideas are racing so fast I can’t hold onto one.\n\nSo… I don’t know. Maybe sometimes I have trouble staying on one point, but I haven’t really thought of it as my thoughts jumping quickly.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you lost interest in being around other people or doing things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying it, because it makes me sound like I’m just being lazy or like I don’t care about people, and I do care. But I’ve definitely pulled back.\n\nI used to sing with the choir, and that was something I actually looked forward to. It wasn’t just singing, it was seeing the same people every week and having a reason to get out and be part of something. And now when it’s time to go, I just… don’t feel it. I’ll think about getting ready, and then it feels like too much, or like I can’t make myself care enough to move. Same with the softball team. We used to have people over sometimes, and I’d help plan food or where everyone would sit, and now the idea of having them in the house feels exhausting before it even starts.\n\nMost days after my shift I come home and I’ll sit at the kitchen table with the planner open. Like I’m trying to be normal and organized — dinner, phone calls, whatever needs doing — but I just stare at it. I can see the things that need to happen, but I can’t seem to start them. Then time goes by and nothing gets done, and my husband ends up picking up the slack.\n\nSo yes, I guess I have lost interest, or maybe lost the ability to get myself to do the things I used to do. It’s not exactly that I hate those things now. It’s more like the feeling that used to push me toward them is just not there most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have other people said that your face or voice seems less expressive or more flat than it used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if anyone has said it exactly that way, like “your voice is flat” or “your face is flat.” My husband maybe says I seem distant, or like I’m not really there in the same way, but I don’t always know what my face is doing.\n\nWhat I do notice is more that I don’t have the same… lift, I guess. Like with choir, I used to enjoy singing, and now I don’t even feel like going. Or having the softball team over — that used to be something I could get into, planning it and being around everybody, and now it just feels like too much.\n\nMost days after my shift, I’ll sit at the kitchen table with the planner open in front of me, and I’ll know I’m supposed to start dinner or make calls or take care of whatever’s written there, but I just sit there. I’m not trying to look blank or sound uninterested. It’s more like I can’t get the feeling to start things, and maybe that comes across on my face or in my voice.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems been going on for six months or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has been longer than six months. It’s hard for me to pin it down exactly because it didn’t feel like one day everything changed, but if I really think about when it started building, it was around when I got put in that temporary dispatch lead role.\n\nThat was about eight months ago, I think. At first I thought it was just the stress of that position and trying to keep up with everything, and I kept telling myself it would settle down or I’d adjust. But looking back, the problems have kind of been there since then, just gradually getting more noticeable. So… yeah, about eight months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think so… not like a voice talking to me when nobody’s there, if that’s what you mean. I mean, at work there are a lot of sounds all the time — radios, engine noise, people talking over each other, dispatch cutting in and out — so sometimes I’ll hear something and I’m not sure if I caught it right. Like I might think I heard my name or a comment, and then I’ll listen harder, but it’s hard to tell with all the background noise.\n\nBut sitting at home or somewhere quiet, I’m not hearing voices or sounds that nobody else hears, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s more that I’m trying to make sense of what people are saying around me, especially over the radio or at work.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Now I’ll start one, then I’m double-checking things, or rereading what I wrote, or trying to figure out if I worded something wrong, and then suddenly I’m behind. And when I give instructions to drivers, I guess I’m not being clear enough, because some of them have complained that what I’m saying is confusing or that I changed something halfway through. I don’t mean to do that, but I can hear myself over-explaining sometimes, and then I lose the main point.\n\nAnd it’s not just work, really. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick). Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. The drivers have complained a few times that when I’m giving instructions, I’m not making sense or I’m saying it in a confusing way. And I hate hearing that, because in my head I feel like I’m trying to be careful and explain it right, but then I guess I over-explain or change how I’m saying it halfway through, and they’re left not knowing what I actually meant.\n\nIt’s also showing up in the route reports. I’ve fallen behind on them because I keep rereading and trying to make the wording clear, and then it takes forever. Those reports used to be pretty routine for me, so it’s embarrassing.\n\nAt home, my husband notices too, not always about “speech” exactly, but about me not keeping up with planning things. He’s had to take over meals, phone calls, basic organizing stuff because I’ll start trying to explain or decide something and then I don’t really get it done. So… yes, I think other people have noticed, even if I didn’t want to admit it at first.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
